 1   EXHIBIT 28
 2
 3   40.350.030 Street and Road Standards

 4          A.    Overview.

 5                1.   Purpose. It is the purpose of this section to establish minimum standards for public and
 6                     private transportation facilities for vehicles, public transit, pedestrians, and bicycles,
 7                     hereafter constructed or improved as a condition of county approval of a development, or
 8                     a transportation project constructed by the county. These standards are intended to preserve
 9                     the community’s quality of life and to minimize total costs over the life of the
10                     transportation facility.

11                2.   Applicability. This section applies to any subdivision, short plat, site plan application, or
12                     conditional use permit; provided, that for the purposes of Sections 40.350.030(B)(4) and
13                     (B)(8), it shall also apply to applications for building permits or other applications for
14                     access to a public road, or to projects within the public right-of-way. Unoccupied utility
15                     and wireless communication facilities shall only be subject to the provisions of Sections
16                     40.350.030(B)(4)(c) – (e) and (B)(8). Private bridges are addressed in Section 40.350.040.

17                3.   Relationship to Comprehensive Plan.

18                     a.   Clark County is required by RCW 36.70A.040(3) to ensure that any development
19                          regulations adopted subsequent to the comprehensive plan “...are consistent with and
20                          implement the comprehensive plan...”

21                     b.   This section is consistent with and implements the goals and policies listed in the
22                          comprehensive plan. Particular attention has been paid to Chapter 5, Transportation
23                          Element.

24                     c.   Interpretations of this section shall be consistent with the effective Arterial Atlas. The
25                          Arterial Atlas identifies all arterials and collectors and specifies the design of these
26                          facilities in general terms.

27                     d.   This section implements the pedestrian and bikeways system plan and the Arterial
28                          Atlas. The atlas requires pedestrian and/or bicycle facilities to be included as part of
29                          certain arterial and collector road cross-sections where the pedestrian and bikeways
30                          system plan indicates such facilities are to be located. This section requires the
31                          inclusion of pedestrian and bikeway facilities in frontage improvements based on the
32                          functional classification adopted in the Arterial Atlas.

33                4.    Functional Classifications – Purpose. The purpose of a functional classification system
34                      for county roads is to define varying levels and types of transportation facilities that
35                      provide for the safe and efficient movement of people and goods, while preserving
36                      residential areas and maintaining the economic vitality of commercial and industrial
37                      areas. The system classifies transportation facilities as either urban or rural roads. Both
38                      urban and rural roads are further divided into arterials, collectors, and access roads. There
39                      is also an urban commercial/industrial category of roads. Existing and proposed arterials
40                      and collectors are shown on the current Arterial Atlas. The county’s functional
41                      classification system for arterials is intended to be in compliance with the federal
42                      classification system.

43                5.    Functional Classifications – Urban Roads. Urban roads are classified as outlined below:



                                                                                                                         190
 1   a.   Arterials.

 2        (1) Parkway Arterial. “Parkway arterial” is the highest classification within the county’s
 3             functional classification system. The purpose of this county road is to carry high
 4             volumes of traffic through the urban area and between major regional activity centers.
 5             This class of roadway is of primary importance in the regional transportation system
 6             as it carries a high proportion of the total urban-area travel. Access is normally
 7             limited to intersections with other arterials. Direct land access is prohibited.

 8        (2) Principal Arterial. “Principal arterial” is the basic element of the county’s road
 9             system. All other functional classifications supplement the principal arterial network.
10             It carries large volumes of traffic over long distances. Access is generally limited to
11             intersections with other arterials and collectors. Signalized intersection spacing is
12             regulated. Direct land access is minimal and managed, but is less restrictive than
13             access from parkway arterials. Spacing is typically two (2) to five (5) miles.

14        (3) Minor Arterial. “Minor arterial” collects and distributes traffic from principal
15             arterials to streets of lower classifications and may allow for traffic to directly access
16             destinations. Minor arterials provide for movement within subareas of the county,
17             whose boundaries are largely defined by principal arterial roadways. They serve
18             through traffic and provide direct access to large commercial, industrial, office and
19             multifamily development but, generally, not to single-family residential properties.
20             Spacing is typically less than two (2) miles.

21   b.   Collectors – Urban Collector. “Urban collector” provides for land access and traffic
22        circulation within and between residential neighborhoods, and commercial and industrial
23        areas. The collector street also collects traffic from local streets and channels it into the
24        arterial system. Direct access to adjacent land uses, however, is still subordinate to traffic
25        movement. Access to abutting properties is controlled through driveway spacing and
26        pavement markings. Typically, collectors are not continuous for any great length,
27        nor do they form a connected network by themselves. Spacing is typically less than
28        two (2) miles.

29   c.   Access Roads.

30        (1) Neighborhood Circulator. “Neighborhood circulator” serves to distribute
31             traffic from collectors and provides direct access for abutting properties.
32             Through trips are discouraged and parking is allowed. In general, these streets
33             connect local access streets to collectors.

34        (2) Local Access. “Local access” streets provide direct access to adjoining
35             properties within a neighborhood. Through trips are discouraged and parking
36             is usually allowed. In general, these streets do not directly connect to arterials
37             or collectors.

38        (3) Short Cul-de-Sac. “Short cul-de-sac” streets are a maximum one hundred fifty
39             (150) feet in length and serve no more than eighteen
40             (18) dwelling units. Parking is allowed. The use of shared driveways off of short
41             cul-de-sacs requires Fire Marshal approval.

42        (4) Alley. “Alley” streets are secondary accesses to the back side of lots. This allows
43             streets at the front of properties not to be encumbered with driveways. Alleys




                                                                                                    191
 1                  are an alternative to frontage access. Parking is not allowed.

 2        d.   Urban Commercial/Industrial. “Urban commercial/industrial” streets serve to
 3             distribute traffic from arterials and provide direct access to abutting commercial or
 4             industrial properties. Through trips are discouraged and parking is optional. Bike
 5             lanes may be required when the projected average daily trips exceed three thousand
 6             (3,000).
 7             A “storefront street” is a pedestrian-oriented street type that can be used in Mixed-
 8             Use and Highway 99 commercial districts where building setbacks are zero (0) to
 9             five (5) feet. Drive-way access is not permitted; wide sidewalks, curb bulb-outs
10             and parking are required.

11   6.   Functional Classifications – Rural Roads. Rural roads are classified as follows:

12        a.   Rural Arterial. “Rural arterial” roads are rural extensions of urban principal arterials
13             and some urban minor arterials. They provide adequate right-of-way for future urban
14             arterial routes. The provision of land access remains subordinate to providing for
15             traffic movement. Parking is not allowed.

16        b.   Collectors.

17             (1) Rural Major Collector. “Rural major collector” roads are rural extensions
18                  of urban minor arterials and some urban collectors. Their primary purpose is to
19                  link rural centers with nearby towns and cities and with state arterial routes.
20                  The provision of land access remains subordinate to providing for traffic
21                  movement. Parking is not allowed.

22             (2) Rural Minor Collector. “Rural minor collector” roads connect local traffic to rural
23                  major collectors and state arterial routes and may be rural extensions of urban minor
24                  arterials or urban collectors. They are spaced so as to be accessible to all developed
25                  areas within the county. The provision of land access is given the same priority as the
26                  provision of traffic movement. Parking is not allowed.

27        c.   Access Roads.

28             (1) Rural Local Access. “Rural local access” roads provide access from parcels to the
29                  rural collector system. Parking is not allowed.

30   7.   Scenic Routes.

31        a.   Scenic routes are roadways with unique scenic or historical features, officially designated
32             by Council. Scenic routes seek to enhance, preserve and facilitate the enjoyment of those
33             scenic or historical features unique to each route.

34        b.   Scenic route design may allow reduced design speed and modified roadway and right-of-
35             way widths to preserve naturally occurring scenic beauty unique to the location of the
36             route. When possible, existing alignment and roadway sections shall be used. Special
37             features, such as vehicle turnouts for vista areas or bicycle/pedestrian facilities, may be
38             provided. Urban or rural collector standards shall be used for right-of-way and roadway
39             sections. The Public Works Director may modify the standards to accommodate unique
40             scenic or historic design considerations.

41        c.   A traffic analysis to determine the impacts on arterials, collectors, and access roads shall




                                                                                                          192
 1                    be completed prior to designating a facility a scenic route.

 2        8.    Urban Reserve, Urban Holding Areas and Rural Centers. The following are special applications
 3              of the functional classifications. Chapter 5 of the comprehensive plan lists additional
 4              transportation improvements required in specific geographic areas.

5               a.    New developments permitted outright within the urban holding and urban reserve areas of
6                     the county shall meet rural road standards, except that the right-of-way for rural local access
7                     roads shall be a minimum of fifty-four
8                     (54) feet to allow for a future neighborhood circulator street.

 9              b.    Compliance with the urban road standards for right-of-way dedication and frontage
10                    improvements shall be required for:
11                   (1) Conditional uses in urban holding areas; and
12                   (2) Conditional uses that are urban in character, within urban reserve areas.
13              c.    Where urban frontage improvements are required and the road to be improved has a
14                    rural classification, Table 40.350.030-1 shall be used to convert rural classifications
15                    to urban.

16              d.    New developments within rural centers shall meet rural road standards. All public
17                    and private roads shall be paved and constructed with sidewalks.
18
                        Table 40.350.030-1. Rural/Urban Classification Conversion
                Rural Classification             Converts to Urban Classification
                Rural Arterial (RA)                             Principal or Minor Arterial
                Rural Major Collector (R-2)                     Minor Arterial or Collector: two
                                                                lanes, center turn lane and bike
                                                                lanes (M-2cb or C-2cb)
                Rural Minor Collector (RM-                      Collector: two lanes (C-2 or C-
                2)                                              2b)
                Rural Local Access                              Neighborhood Circulator

19   B.   Standards for Development Review.

20        1.   Transportation Impact Study. The requirements for a transportation impact study are
21             stated in Section 40.350.020(D).

22        2.   Circulation Plan.

23             a.    Purpose and Applicability. The purpose of this section is to ensure adequate cross-
24                   circulation in a manner which allows subsequent developments to meet these
25                   standards, and to provide a mechanism for integrating various streets into an efficient
26                   and safe transportation network.
27                   Developments that are required to conduct a transportation impact study or
28                   construct frontage improvements shall meet the requirements of this section.

29             b.    Information Requirements for a Circulation Plan. Applicants shall submit a circulation
30                   plan which includes the subject site and all adjacent parcels. Proposed streets must be
31                   shown to the point of connection with the existing street system within six hundred



                                                                                                                193
 1        (600) feet. The circulation plan shall demonstrate feasibility with development of
 2        adjacent properties, or may revise the off-site portion of prior approved plans.
 3        Circulation plans shall also be consistent with the Arterial Atlas, as amended. A
 4        circulation plan shall be submitted at application. Draft circulation plans may be
 5        submitted at preapplication.
 6        (1) Information Requirements for Developments in Urban Area. Urban circulation plans
 7              shall be schematic in nature and to an engineering scale (e.g., 1" = 100’, 1" = 200’, 1"
 8              = 400’). The plan should include sufficient off-site and on-site conditions to evaluate
 9              it against the review criteria. It shall include:

10              (a) Proposed project boundary;
11              (b) Existing and proposed streets, transit routes and facilities, and other
12                   pedestrian/bicycle destinations within six hundred (600) feet of the project
13                   boundary;

14              (c) Site access points for vehicles, pedestrians, bicycles, and transit; and
15              (d) Sensitive lands (wetlands, shoreline, geologic hazard, floodplain, etc.), if they
16                    are contained in the county’s information package.
17              The circulation plan should be prepared on eight and one-half (8½) inch by eleven
18              (11) inch (8½" x 11") or eleven (11) inch by seventeen (17) inch (11" x 17") or
19              twenty-four (24) inch by thirty-six (36) inch (24" x 36") format, and can be
20              superimposed on the “arterials, C-Tran routes, parks and trails” and “elevation
21              contours” pages provided with the developer’s GIS packet. Additional explanation
22              or an additional legend may be required to adequately show proposed on-site
23              facilities.

24        (2) Information Requirements for Developments in Rural Area. Rural circulation plans
25              shall be schematic in nature and based on the appropriate quarter-section map. The
26              plan should include sufficient on-site and off-site conditions to evaluate it against the
27              review criteria. Rural circulation plans shall include:

28              (a) Proposed project boundary;
29              (b) How the project site connects to the existing street system;
30              (c) Any arterials identified in the Arterial Atlas, as amended, within eight hundred
31                   (800) feet of the site.

32   c.   Review Criteria for an Urban Circulation Plan.

33        (1) Cross-Circulation. Cross-circulation shall be provided in a manner that meets these
34              standards and, where possible, that will allow subsequent developments to meet these
35              standards:

36             (a)   Block Length. Block lengths shall be between one hundred
37                   (100) to eight hundred (800) feet; provided, that where a block is partially
38                   defined by an arterial or industrial road the block lengths along the arterial
39                   shall be no less than the minimum full
40                   access intersection spacing specified in Table 40.350.030-3. For the
41                   purpose of this section, “block length” is the distance between public




                                                                                                  194
 1                           road intersections as measured along the nearside right-of-way line.

 2                     (b) Block Perimeter. The block perimeter shall not exceed three thousand
 3                           two hundred (3,200) feet unless accessway(s) for pedestrian or bicycle
 4                           circulation are provided or where topographic or other physical
 5                           constraints preclude achieving this standard.

 6              (2) Access Street System. The access street system shall:
 7                     (a) Provide convenient parcel access to and from adjacent arterials and/or
 8                           collectors;

 9                     (b) Be designed to discourage external traffic from short-cutting;
10                     (c) Be designed to discourage vehicular speeds in excess of legal speed
11                           limits;

12                     (d) Be designed for convenient circulation of internal traffic without reliance
13                           on the arterial systems;

14                     (e) Support direct travel by pedestrians, bicyclists, and transit users; and
15                     (f)   Discourage unnecessary streets and hard surfaces.

16   3.   Transportation Design Criteria. The design criteria set out in Tables 40.350.030-2 and
17        40.350.030-3 are adopted as a portion of the Clark County standard specifications. Such
18        criteria are applicable to roads located within and adjacent to a development. These criteria
19        are intended for normal conditions. The responsible official may require higher standards
20        for unusual site conditions.
21        All urban roads except alleys consist of a core road section and a flex zone section.

22        a.    The core road consists of the traveled-way portion of the road, as well as medians and
23              turning lanes on higher classification roads. Core road features as shown on the
24              Standard Details Manual drawings allow little, if any, variation unless a road
25              modification request is approved.

26              (1) Travel and turning lanes require impervious pavement on all rural roads, and
27                    urban arterials, collectors, industrial/commercial, and neighborhood circulator
28                    roads.
29               (2) Permanent median areas may utilize stormwater low impact development
30                    features including, but not limited to, bioretention swales and permeable
31                    pavement. Such features shall be subject toapproval by the Public Works
32                    Director and shall be designed to ensure adequate public safety.

33         b.    The flex zone consists of that portion of the roadway outside of the core road. Flex zone
34               features can include stormwater best management practice features, parking and bike
35               lanes, sidewalks, and planter and utility strips, depending on the road classification. These
36               features may be designed with considerable flexibility subject to engineering approval by
37               the county; however, all features applicable to the road classification shall be provided.
38               Some flex zone features may require more right-of-way than is noted in Table 40.350.030-
39               2.

40               (1) Stormwater Features. Stormwater low impact development features as found in the



                                                                                                          195
 1       Clark County Stormwater Manual are allowed with approval from the Public Works
 2       Director. Stormwater features shall be designed and constructed to ensure adequate
 3       public safety. Right-of-way in excess of that required in Table 40.350.030-2 may be
 4       needed to accommodate stormwater features.

 5       (a) Permeable Pavement. Permeable pavement may be used for the following:
 6             (i)   Sidewalks and separated bike lanes on all road classifications;

 7             (ii) Parking and nonseparated bike lanes of all urban access roads, including
 8                   neighborhood circulators; and

 9             (iii) Travel lanes of all urban access roads except neighborhood circulators.
10             (iv) Prior to acceptance of permeable pavement surfaces by the county, the
11                   applicant shall sign a Permeable Pavement Testing and Acceptance
12                   Agreement in a form acceptable by the Public Works Director.

13       (b) Infiltration Systems. Infiltration systems such as bioretention systems, rain
14             gardens and infiltration planters are subject to the following limitations:

15             (i)   Infiltration from stormwater features shall not threaten the safety or
16                   integrity of underground utilities. The Public Works Director shall require
17                   that infiltration features located in close proximity to utilities be designed
18                   to accommodate the utilities;

19             (ii) Infiltration from stormwater features shall not adversely affect the
20                   function and integrity of the core road.

21   (2) On-Street Parking. Where required, on-street parking shall be
22       subject to the following:

23        (a) Angled “head-in” parking may be allowed with approval of the Public
24             Works Director; provided, that backing over sidewalks or bicycle lanes
25             is prohibited;

26        (b) Curb extensions containing bioretention facilities are allowed in parking
27             lanes subject to the following:

28             (i)   Curb extensions shall not extend more than seven (7) feet into the
29                   parking lane, and shall not interrupt required bike lanes.

30             (ii) On commercial/industrial roads, the length of mid-block curb
31                   extensions is limited to twenty percent (20%) of the curb length of
32                   a block, or in the case of a partial block development, the road
33                   frontage of the site.

34             (iii) Mid-block curb extensions are prohibited on arterials, collectors and
35                   store-front roads.

36             (iv) Mid-block curb extensions on all urban access roads, including
37                   neighborhood circulators, are allowed with restrictions in residential
38                   subdivisions under one (1) of the following options:




                                                                                              196
 1                     [a] Curb extensions can occupy up to ten percent (10%) of the curb
 2                          length of a block, or in the case of a partial block development,
 3                          the road frontage of the site;

 4                     [b] Curb extensions can occupy up to twenty-five percent (25%)
 5                          of the curb length of a block, or in the case of a partial block
 6                          development, the road frontage of the site; provided, that the
 7                          applicant demonstrates on the plat that fifty (50) foot long two
 8                          (2) way passing opportunities are provided at one hundred fifty
 9                          (150) foot maximum intervals along the roadway for urban
10                          local access roads. Acceptable methods include the use of
11                          abutting driveways, twenty (20) foot wide travel-way clear
12                          zones, or other methods as may be approved by the Public
13                          Works Director; or

14                     [c] Curb extensions can occupy more than twenty-five percent
15                          (25%) of the block length; provided, that the applicant
16                          demonstrates on the plat that at least one (1) on-street parking
17                          space per lot is maintained. Single on-street parallel parking
18                          spaces between driveways shall be at least seventeen (17) feet
19                          in length, measured from five (5) feet from the top of the slope
20                          of the driveway wing or end of the curb radius. Two
21                          (2) or more consecutive on-street parallel parking spaces shall
22                          require two (2) additional feet between each space.

23            (v) Curb extensions up to fifty (50) feet long are allowed at intersections of
24                  local access roads and short cul-de-sacs, or combinations thereof. Curb
25                  extensions at intersections may be required for storefront streets, subject
26                  to Section
27                  9.1.2 of the Highway 99 Overlay District Standards in Appendix F to
28                  Title 40, or for mixed-use developments. Such extensions shall not count
29                  toward the mid-block percentage requirements in this subsection.
30                  Approval of such curb extensions shall require demonstration of adequate
31                  vehicular turning movement capability.

32            (vi) Measurements. The length of mid-block curb extensions shall be measured
33                  between the tangents of the standard curb. The length of curb extensions at
34                  intersections shall be measured between the tangent of the standard curb
35                  and the face of the curb of the intersecting street. “Block length” is the
36                  distance between road intersections as measured along the nearside right-
37                  of-way or easement line.

38   (3) Bike Lanes. Bike lanes, when required by the road classification, are subject to the
39        following:

40        (a) Bike lanes may abut travel lanes or be located outside of the curb line,
41             depending on the feasibility of existing and future connections.

42        (b) When combined with pedestrian paths, the combined width shall be a minimum
43             of ten (10) feet wide.

44        (c) Bike lanes shall be continuous through a development, i.e., cannot be



                                                                                                197
 1              interrupted by curb extensions or other features.

 2   (4) Sidewalks. Sidewalks shall be provided on both sides of all public streets in urban
 3        areas and within rural centers, subject to the following:

 4        (a) Attached and detached sidewalk widths shall be as noted in Table 40.350.030-
 5              2.

 6        (b) When combined with bike lanes, the combined width shall be at least ten (10)
 7              feet.

 8        (c) Sidewalks shall be continuous through a development, i.e., cannot be
 9              interrupted by curb extensions or other features.
10        (d) Other than for sidewalks in rural center zones where the standard right-of-way
11              dedication is insufficient, sidewalks may be allowed within easements only
12              where it is demonstrated that such sidewalks cannot be located within the
13              established public right-of-way or private roadway tract due to natural features
14              (e.g., significant trees, rock outcroppings, steep topography, etc.) that should be
15              preserved, or irregular lot configuration.

16   (5) Utilities. For new developments, the Public Works Director shall require that
17        underground utilities be designed to accommodate infiltration features that are in close
18        proximity to the utilities.

19   (6) Curb and gutter, when required, shall be a minimum of eighteen (18) inches wide. When
20        curbs are required, vertical curb is required for all roads except for cul-de-sac bulbs.

                                                                                                                                                                                                                           202
 1   4.   Access Management.

 2        a.   Applicability. As noted in Section 40.350.030(A)(2), this subsection also applies to
 3             applications for building permits and applications for access to public roads.
 4             Additionally, paving of driveways in the urban and rural areas shall follow the
 5             requirements of Section 40.350.030(B)(7)(c).

 6        b.   Access to Local Access Roads.

 7             (1) Driveway Spacing.
 8                   (a) Excepting the bulbs of cul-de-sacs, driveways providing access onto
 9                         nonarterial and noncollector streets serving single-family or duplex
10                         residential structures shall be located a minimum of five (5) feet from an
11                         interior side property line or zero (0) feet as a shared driveway approach.
12                         Where two (2) driveways are permitted, a minimum separation of fifty
13                         (50) feet shall be required between the driveways, measured from near
14                         edge to near edge.

15                   (b) Corner lot driveways shall be a minimum of forty (40) feet from the
16                         projected curb line or edge of pavement, as measured to the nearest edge
17                         of the driveway, or in cases where this may be impractical, the driveway
18                         may be limited to twenty (20) feet in width and located five (5) feet from
19                         the property line away from the intersection or as a twenty-five (25) foot
20                         wide shared driveway at this property line. Where a residential corner lot
21                         is located at the intersection of a nonarterial or noncollector street with an
22                         arterial street, the corner clearance requirements of Section
23                         40.350.030(B)(4)(c)(2)(f) shall apply to the nonarterial or noncollector
24                         street.

25                   (c) Existing flag lots and joint driveways serving between two (2) and four
26                         (4) lots are exempt from the requirements of this subsection.

27                   (d) Nonresidential driveways are prohibited from taking access from an
28                         urban access road as defined in Table 40.350.030-2 unless no access
29                         exists or can be provided to a collector.

30             (2) Driveway Number and Width. A maximum of two (2) driveways may be
31                   permitted to a residential lot or individual duplex unit meeting the spacing
32                   requirements of Section 40.350.030(B)(4)(b). Joint-use driveways may be
33                   allowed and will count as a driveway for each residential lot or duplex unit. For
34                   a joint-use driveway, a minimum of a twenty (20) foot wide easement is
35                   required. Driveways shall have a minimum width of twelve (12) feet of clear,
36                   unobstructed all-weather driving surface and an overhead clearance of thirteen
37                   (13) feet, six (6) inches. The first, or only, driveway shall be twelve (12) feet to
38                   thirty-five (35) feet in width. If a second driveway is allowed, the maximum
39                   width of the second driveway will be fifteen (15) feet.

40             (3) Driveway Length.
41                  (a) All portions of shared driveways off of short cul-de-sac roads shall be within
42                        three hundred (300) feet of the intersection of the short cul-de-sac with a higher
43                        classified road as measured to the intersecting street’s face of curb; provided,



                                                                                                            203
 1            that such driveways are subject to Fire Marshal approval.

 2       (b) All new modified or extended driveways longer than three hundred (300) feet
 3            shall be provided with an approved turnaround at the terminus. There shall also
 4            be approved turnouts constructed such that the maximum distance from turnout
 5            to turnout, or from turnout to turnarounds, does not exceed five hundred (500)
 6            feet. Turnouts shall comply with the Standard Details Manual. Construction of
 7            roads and driveways within the wild land urban interface/intermix area shall
 8            conform to Section 15.13.030.

 9   (4) Maximum Dwelling Units Served by Access Roads.
10        (a) No road may serve more than one hundred (100) lots or dwelling units unless
11             that road is connected by a second vehicle access road to the same “feeder” road
12             at a different location, or to another “feeder” road that functions at a level equal
13             to at least an urban local residential access road or a rural local access road. The
14             second vehicle access road may be a county emergency-only access road, if it
15             serves less than two hundred
16             (200) lots or dwelling units.
17             The second access may be satisfied through the use of an existing roadway
18             network in the existing adjacent neighborhood if:

19             (i)   An existing road was previously stubbed indicating intent for future
20                   access; or

21             (ii) An easement has been dedicated specifically for such purpose, and a
22                   roadway has been built to county standards or will be constructed with the
23                   development to county standards.
24                   In either case, the increase in traffic volume on the existing roadway
25                   network must not cause the traffic volume to exceed the design volume
26                   of the existing roadway network.
27        (b) Urban neighborhood circulator roads within a development which meet
28             the access requirements above may serve up to three hundred (300) lots
29             or units if approved by the review authority. However, the review
30             authority may require a traffic circulation study showing a balanced
31             traffic flow of less than two thousand (2,000) vehicles per day past any
32             dwelling unit accessing on a neighborhood circulator road or lesser
33             classification upon full buildout.

34        (c) When required emergency or regular secondary access roads cannot be
35             installed due to location on property, topography, waterways,
36             nonnegotiable grades or other similar conditions, the County Fire
37             Marshal may require additional fire protection as specified in Title 15.

38        (d) The standards contained in Section 40.350.030(B)(4)(b)(4) are waived in
39             their entirety for developments in rural areas.

40   (5) Exceptions.
41       (a) The review authority may grant an exception to the requirements of
42            Section 40.350.030(B)(4)(b)(3) to extend the maximum distance between
43            turnouts/turn-arounds or allow other appropriate relief where it is




                                                                                            204
 1                   impractical or excessively costly to meet these requirements due to
 2                   topography, sensitive areas, natural features, or where application of these
 3                   standards would be disproportional.

 4             (b) The review authority may grant an exception to the requirements of
 5                   Section 40.350.030(B)(4)(b)(4)(a) in the case of a subdivision with more
 6                   than one (1) phase, when it can be shown that the other necessary access
 7                   roads will be constructed in a future phase of the same subdivision. Street
 8                   stubs built to the property line of property not under the developer’s
 9                   control do not qualify for such an exception.

10   c.   Access to Collectors.

11        (1) In order to limit the number of residential roads intersecting with collectors
12              while providing adequate neighborhood circulation, residential roads
13              intersecting with collectors shall be classified and constructed to standards
14              applicable to local residential access roads unless the review authority finds
15              that a lesser classification adequately provides for the circulation needs of the
16              surrounding area. Road approach permits not associated with development
17              shall be reviewed using a Type I process.

18        (2) Driveways.
19             (a) Urban Collectors. No residential driveways in the urban area
20                   will be permitted to access collectors unless no other access to the site exists or
21                   can be made available.

22             (b) Rural Collectors. Residential driveways in the rural area will not be permitted to
23                   access collectors if direct lot access is available to an existing rural access road
24                   as defined in Tables 40.350.030-2 and 40.350.030-3.

25             (c) Spacing. When driveways on collectors are permitted, they shall be spaced in
26                   accordance with Table 40.350.030-4. The distance between adjacent one (1)
27                   way driveways with the inbound drive upstream from the outbound drive may
28                   be one-half (½) the distance shown. Where raised channelization exists, only
29                   those driveways on the development side of the road will be considered for
30                   minimum separation requirements.

31             (d) Number of Driveways. The number of driveways and driveway lanes shall be
32                   based upon an estimate of site traffic generation in accordance with Table
33                   40.350.030-5. Multiple driveways are not permitted until the estimated ADT
34                   exceeds the number shown in the second column for the different types of land
35                   use. Then, an additional driveway is allowed each time the estimated ADT
36                   increases above the previous maximum ADT for each driveway as shown in the
37                   third column; provided, that the additional driveways meet the spacing
38                   requirements specified in Table 40.350.030-4. Two (2) driveway exit lanes are
39                   allowed when the ADT exceeds seven hundred (700).

40              (e) Width. A single-family residential driveway onto a collector shall be fifteen
41                   (15) to thirty-five (35) feet in width; provided, that a joint-use driveway serving
42                   two (2) residential lots shall not exceed thirty-six (36) feet in width. A
43                   nonresidential two




                                                                                                    205
 1                   (2) way driveway onto a collector shall be twenty-four (24) to forty (40) feet
 2                   in width.

 3             (f)   Corner Clearance. To provide adequate corner clearance, the tangent curb
 4                   length between the nearest edge of a driveway on an intersecting side street and
 5                   a collector roadway, or a driveway on a collector roadway and an intersection
 6                   with a cross street, shall be fifty (50) feet. Where the intersection is signalized
 7                   or is planned for signalization, driveways shall be limited to right-turn
 8                   movements if located within one hundred twenty-five (125) feet of a collector
 9                   or as provided for in Section 40.350.030(B)(4)(d)(2).

10             (g) Temporary Driveway. A temporary driveway may be allowed when, due to
11                   conditions beyond the control of the applicant, minimum driveway separation
12                   cannot be achieved at the time of application. The review authority may approve
13                   a temporary
14                    driveway when an access plan shows future removal of the temporary
15                    driveway and a new driveway which meets the spacing standards
16                    shown above.

17        (3) Additional Improvements. The installation of other improvements such as left-
18             turn lanes, right-turn lanes and traffic signals may be required by the County
19             Engineer where found necessary on the basis of a traffic engineering study. The
20             study shall address operations of the driveway or intersection, including the
21             adequacy of vehicular turning movements.

22   d.   Access to Arterials. In order to limit the number of residential roads intersecting with
23        arterials while providing adequate neighborhood circulation, residential roads
24        intersecting with urban arterials shall be classified and constructed to standards
25        applicable to local residential access or collector roads unless the review authority
26        finds that a lesser classification adequately provides for the circulation needs of the
27        surrounding area. In those cases in which an urban access street is less than thirty-
28        six (36) feet wide, such street shall have a minimum width of thirty-six (36) feet at
29        the intersection with the arterial and shall be tapered according to accepted
30        engineering practices and supplemental standards in Section 40.350.030(C)(1)(b) as
31        determined by the Public Works Director. Road approach permits not associated
32        with development shall be reviewed using a Type I process.

33        (1) Driveways. No driveways will be permitted to access onto urban or rural
34             arterials unless no other access to the site exists or can be provided.

35             (a) Spacing. When driveways on arterials are permitted, they shall be spaced
36                   in accordance with Table 40.350.030-4.

37             (b) Number of Driveways. Where permitted, the number of driveways and
38                   driveway lanes on arterials shall be based upon an estimate of site traffic
39                   generation in accordance with Table 40.350.030-6.
40                   (i)   Multiple driveways are not permitted until the estimated ADT
41                         exceeds the number shown in the second column for the different
42                         type of land use. Then, an additional driveway is allowed each
43                         time the estimated ADT increases above the previous maximum
44                         ADT for each driveway as shown in the columns for minor
45                         arterials and principal arterials; provided, that the additional



                                                                                                     206
 1                   driveways meet the spacing requirements specified in Table
 2                   40.350.030-4. As an example, a commercial land use on a minor
 3                   arterial has one (1) driveway up to two thousand (2,000) ADT,
 4                   then two (2) driveways for two thousand one (2,001) to five
 5                   thousand five hundred (5,500) ADT,three (3) driveways for five
 6                   thousand five hundred one (5,501) to nine thousand (9,000)
 7                   ADT, and so on.

 8             (ii) A permit for exclusive use of a truck driveway in addition to the nontruck
 9                   traffic may be granted for commercial uses that exceed thirty thousand
10                   (30,000) square feet of gross floor space.

11             (iii) Two (2) driveway exit lanes are allowed when the ADT exceeds one
12                   thousand (1,000).

13        (c) Width. A single-family residential driveway onto an urban arterial shall be
14             fifteen (15) to thirty-five (35) feet in width; provided, that a joint-use driveway
15             serving two (2) residential lots shall not exceed thirty-six (36) feet in width. A
16             commercial and multifamily two (2) way driveway onto an arterial shall be
17             twenty-four (24) to forty (40) feet in width.

18        (d) Corner Clearance. To provide adequate corner clearance, the tangent curb
19             length between the nearest edge of a driveway on an intersecting side street and
20             an arterial roadway, or a driveway on an arterial roadway and an intersection
21             with a cross street, shall be fifty (50) feet. Where the intersection is signalized
22             or is planned for signalization, driveways shall be limited to right-turn
23             movements if located within two hundred fifty (250) feet on minor and principal
24             arterials or as provided for in Section 40.350.030(B)(4)(d)(2).

25        (e) Temporary Driveway. A temporary driveway may be allowed when, due to
26             temporary conditions beyond the control of the applicant, minimum driveway
27             separation cannot be achieved at the time of application. The review authority
28             may approve a temporary driveway when an access plan shows future removal
29             of the temporary driveway and a new driveway which meets the spacing
30             standards shown above is assured to be constructed.

31   (2) Additional Improvements. The installation of other improvements such as left-turn
32        lanes, right-turn lanes and traffic signals may be required by the County Engineer
33        where found necessary on the basis of a traffic engineering study. The study shall
34        address operations of the driveway or intersection, including the adequacy of
35        vehicular turning movements.

36   (3) Medians and Channelization Policy. In order to preserve capacity and promote
37        safety, urban arterials shall generally include raised medians to restrict cross
38        traffic movements. In general, full-access intersections, signalized and
39        nonsignalized, on arterials will be permitted only with other county, state and
40        city roads as are designated on the Arterial Atlas, as amended. Circulation from
41        such intersections in most cases will satisfy the access needs of adjacent land.
42        The County Engineer is solely responsible for decisions regarding the
43        placement of raised medians on county roads. Any decision by the county that
44        a raised median will not be installed on an arterial will require a specific
45        determination by the County Engineer that (a) the roadway will operate safely



                                                                                             207
 1               without a raised median and (b) that the roadway will operate at the required
 2               capacity without a raised median. Other factors to be considered by the County
 3               Engineer include, but are not limited to, the traffic volumes and speeds on the
 4               roadway, the crash history on the roadway, roadway geometric factors and
 5               other characteristics, and the adequacy of sight lines and distances.
 6               Additionally, the number of fronting driveways, the traffic volumes on those
 7               driveways, the likely adverse impacts on fronting and other properties, the
 8               extent of access control along the arterial, and the number and location of other
 9               accesses available to those properties will be considered.

10               For commercial zones, raised medians will not be required on arterials where
11               the average weekday traffic is projected to be less than twenty thousand
12               (20,000) vehicles per day over the next five (5) year period and where the
13               County Engineer makes the determination that the roadway will operate safely
14               and without operational problems.

15   e.     Access to State Routes. If the access serving a development is onto a state road or
16          highway, required dedication and/or improvements thereto must meet the
17          requirements of the Washington Department of Transportation.

 2   5.   Frontage Roads/Improvement.

 3        a.   General Requirement. Unless already fully developed to the transportation standards and
 4             subject to the limitations set forth in this section and in Sections 40.350.030(B)(15) and
 5             40.550.010, a partial-width road shall be established and constructed to the applicable right-
 6             of-way or easement and improvement standards set out in Section
 7             40.350.030 to that portion of a frontage public or private road which abuts a parcel being
 8             developed as a condition of development approval.

 9             (1) The right-of-way or easement width shall be a minimum of one-half (½) of that
10                  specified in Tables 40.350.030-2 and 40.350.030-3; provided, that such minimum
11                  width may be increased where necessary to accommodate the minimum roadway
12                  improvement provided below to allow a minimum three (3) feet of right-of-way
13                  beyond the back of the sidewalk for urban public roads unless the sidewalk is
14                  detached from the curb with sufficient room to provide for utilities and signing, or for
15                  needed construction clearance, slopes or other features.
16                  In the case of a development containing an urban arterial or fronting on an urban
17                  arterial street, the developer shall only be required to construct improvements up
18                  to forty-four (44) feet in width, or twenty-two (22) feet on a partial-width frontage,
19                  together with curbs and sidewalks, unless a wider section is necessary to
20                  accommodate




                                                                                                      209
 1             the development.
 2             Sufficient right-of-way and easement for a partial-width road must be
 3             provided to accommodate all necessary appurtenances required for
 4             construction including, but not limited to, approved cut or fill slopes or
 5             retaining structures if needed. If sufficient right-of-way is not available, slope
 6             easements from neighboring properties are an acceptable alternative. Such
 7             easements shall be recorded with the final plat. The county may require the
 8             proposed road cross-sections showing neighboring topography be submitted in
 9             order to determine if the partial-width road can be constructed as required.

10        (2) Partial-width roadways shall be a minimum of twenty (20) feet wide; provided,
11             that     curb      extensions,      when        allowed   under     Section
12             40.350.030(B)(3)(b)(2)(b)(iv)(c), may reduce the minimum traveled way width
13             on urban local roads to no less than sixteen (16) feet.

14        (3) New partial-width roads will be allowed in commercial, office or industrial
15             developments only after a traffic study verifies the adequacy of the roadway
16             for clearance and turning movements.

17        (4) Where physical obstructions or development constraints preclude or limit full
18             completion of the frontage road on the abutting property, the partial-width
19             roads may be allowed.

20        (5) Parking shall be prohibited along partial-width roads, with signs and pavement
21             markings being the responsibility of the developer unless the partial-width road
22             is at least twenty-four (24) feet wide to provide a clear sixteen (16) foot wide
23             traveled way in addition to an eight (8) foot wide parking lane.

24        (6) Where frontage improvements are required, the county will perform pavement
25             deflection testing to determine the adequacy of the existing pavement. Where
26             remaining life of the pavement is less than five (5) years, the developer shall
27             construct the roadway to current standards to the centerline or twenty-two (22)
28             feet, whichever is less. If remaining life is greater than five (5) years, the road
29             shall be cut back to a location where the structure is sound and the widening
30             constructed. However, in no case shall the reconstruction be less than four (4)
31             feet in width from the existing edge of pavement to the new edge of pavement
32             or face of curb. The county may require reconstruction to the centerline or
33             twenty-two
34             (22) feet, whichever is less, if the review authority determines the geometrics
35             or other existing features are inadequate.

36   b.   Exceptions.

37        (1) The right-of-way and easement requirements of Section 40.350.030(B)(5)(a)
38             shall not apply to the construction, remodeling or enlargement of any Group R-
39             Division 3 (single-family or duplex) or Group U occupancy (as defined in the
40             International Building Code), to the construction of any accessory residential
41             structure, to any sign, or to the structural addition, alteration or repair to any
42             existing structure within any twelve (12) month period which neither exceeds
43             twenty-five percent (25%) of the value of the existing structure nor increases
44             the total floor space of the structure by more than ten percent (10%).




                                                                                                     210
 1             (2) The roadway frontage improvement requirements of Section 40.350.030(B)(5)(a)
 2                  shall not apply to:

 3                  (a) Rural developments not located within rural centers; or
 4                  (b) Urban or rural center developments which the County Engineer finds, based
 5                         upon an engineering traffic study, will not result in an increase of total site trip
 6                         generations during the p.m. peak hour of more than ten percent (10%);
 7                         provided, that such otherwise exempt developments shall be required to make
 8                         intersection and sight distance improvements in accordance with Sections
 9                         40.350.030(B)(7) and (8) and such frontage road improvements as are necessary
10                         in order to provide minimally safe access to the development.

11        c.   Deferral.

12             (1) In the event that required frontage road improvements are included as a portion of a
13                  county road project on the county’s six (6) year transportation improvement program
14                  scheduled to be undertaken within six (6) years, the developer, in lieu of constructing
15                  or guaranteeing the construction pursuant to Section 40.350.030(C)(4)(i) of such
16                  frontage improvements, may be permitted to contribute a proportionate share towards
17                  the cost of such county road project by an agreement consistent with the requirements
18                  of RCW 82.02.020.

19             (2) The development approval authority may defer frontage road improvements, in
20                  whole or in part, where the current development proposal is for multiple lots in the
21                  R1-5, R1-6, R1-7.5, R1-10 or R1-20 zoning districts larger than one (1) acre and a
22                  covenant running with the land is recorded requiring such improvements to be
23                  undertaken when redivision is proposed at an urban density.

24             (3) The development approval authority may defer frontage road improvements, in
25                  whole or in part, where the development proposal is located in a rural center, and a
26                  covenant running with the land is recorded requiring the owner to contribute their
27                  share to a larger road or frontage improvement project when undertaken by the
28                  county. For the purposes of this subsection, “share” of the cost of deferred frontage
29                  improvements includes the then-current cost of engineering and frontage
30                  improvements required at preliminary development approval divided by the
31                  number of lots. Deferrals of improvements under this subsection may be denied
32                  when:

33                 (a) Conditions are, or will be caused to be, inadequate to provide a minimum
34                         level of service as specified in Section 40.350.020; or

35                 (b) A significant traffic or safety hazard would be caused or materially
36                         aggravated by the proposed development; or

37                 (c) The development must provide safe walking conditions per RCW
38                         58.17.110.

39   6.   Off-Site Road Improvement.

40        a.   General. Nothing in this section shall be construed to preclude denial of a proposed
41             development where off-site road conditions are inadequate to provide a minimum
42             level of service as specified in Section 40.350.020 or a significant traffic or safety



                                                                                                        211
 1        hazard would be caused or materially aggravated by the proposed development;
 2        provided, that the applicant may voluntarily agree to mitigate such direct impacts in
 3        accordance with the provisions of RCW 82.02.020.

 4   b.   Requirements for Off-Site Access Road Improvements. All roads providing access
 5        to parcels being developed, whether such roads are to be public or private, shall at a
 6        minimum:

 7        (1) Within the urban area, have an unobstructed and paved roadway width of
 8             twenty (20) feet, or eighteen (18) feet of pavement with one
 9             (1) foot wide shoulders. Any preexisting roadway narrower than eighteen (18)
10             feet with one (1) foot shoulders shall be widened to the full twenty (20) foot
11             standard. Those portions of a roadway reduced in width due to curb
12             extensions allowed under Section 40.350.030(B)(5)(a)(2) are not required to
13             meet the above requirement, provided a minimum of sixteen (16) feet of
14             pavement is provided.

15        (2) Within the rural area, off-site public access roads shall meet the same standards
16             as Section 40.350.030(B)(6)(b)(1). Off-site private access roads are not
17             required to be paved but shall have an all-weather driving surface, with an
18             unobstructed roadway width of twenty (20) feet; except in those cases where
19             the preexisting road is eighteen (18) feet wide, additional widening to twenty
20             (20) feet is not necessary. Any preexisting roadway narrower than eighteen (18)
21             feet shall be widened to the full twenty (20) foot standard.

22        (3) Have an unobstructed vertical clearance of not less than thirteen (13) feet six (6)
23             inches (13’ 6").
24        (4) Notwithstanding the foregoing, roads providing access to legal lots created prior to
25             July 9, 1996, being developed with a Group R-Division 3 structure or residential
26             mobile home, may be constructed with a minimum twelve (12) foot wide
27             unobstructed all-weather driving surface.

28        (5) Off-site private roads providing access to lots being developed shall have a minimum
29             easement partial width of twenty-five (25) feet from such lot to a public road.

30   c.   Requirements for Off-Site Intersection Improvement. The owners of a parcel being
31        developed shall enter into a signal participation agreement to contribute a proportionate
32        share towards the cost of a traffic signal when:

33        (1) An intersection impacted by the proposed development is designated by the county for
34             installation of a traffic signal; and

35        (2) The parcel being developed is not located within a traffic impact fee (TIF) service
36             area; and

37        (3) During the peak hour, the development generates a minimum of three percent (3%)
38             increase of traffic on the intersection approach leg impacted by the development, or
39             five (5) trips on a minor leg (those legs of the intersection that have the smaller
40             approach volume), or twenty (20) trips on a major leg (those legs of the intersection
41             that have the larger approach volumes); and

42        (4) The peak hour level of service at the leg of the intersection impacted by the site-
43             generated traffic is at or will fall below the minimum level of service standard for



                                                                                                    212
 1                    that intersection as defined in Section 40.350.020.

 2   7.    Intersection Design.

 3         a.   Intersection Geometry. Private and public roads shall be laid out so as to intersect at an
 4              angle as near to a right angle as practicable, but in no case less than seventy-five (75)
 5              degrees for roads intersecting collectors and arterials and no less than sixty (60) degrees
 6              for access roads, unless modified pursuant to Section 40.550.010. Opposing roads
 7              accessing an intersection shall either be aligned or will be separated by a minimum
 8              intersection spacing, as specified in Table 40.350.030-3. Depending on the width of the
 9              intersection opening, an offset greater than ten (10) feet for access roads or five (5) feet for
10              collectors and arterials is not allowed.

11         b.   Intersection Right-of-Way. Intersections shall have a minimum corner radius of ten (10)
12              feet along the right-of-way lines for access roads and a minimum corner radius of twenty-
13              five (25) feet along the right-of-way lines for collectors and arterials, unless road
14              improvements require a greater radius.
15              On collectors and arterials, the dedication of right-of-way on corners shall include
16              the chord of the radius. The county will accept an easement for this chord instead
17              of dedication of right-of-way. For arterials intersecting with other arterials, an
18              additional six (6) feet of right-of-way may be required on both sides of the
19              roadway if a future turning lane is required, based on transportation impact study,
20              within twenty (20) years from the time an application is submitted. The length of
21              the additional right-of-way shall be determined based on the transportation impact
22              study.

23         c.   Paving of Intersecting Area. Where connecting to a paved street, whether public or
24              private, the connecting road or driveway (excluding driveways in rural area) shall
25              be paved twenty-five (25) feet back from the nearest edge of the traveled lane, or
26              shall be equal to the minimum intersection radii as specified in Table 40.350.030-3,
27              whichever is greater.
28              Driveways in rural areas connecting with paved public roads shall be paved from
29              the edge of the public road to the right-of-way or to twenty
30              (20) feet from the edge, whichever is greater.
31              Rural paving shall be done in accordance with the equivalent base structural
32              requirements of the gravel road section as noted in the Standard Details Manual.

33   8.   Sight Distances. As noted in subsection (A)(2) of this section, this subsection also applies
34        to applications for building permits and applications for access to public roads. Unless
35        modified pursuant to Section 40.550.010, public and private roads shall comply with the
36        following sight distance requirements:

37        a.    Stopping Sight Distance. Intersection sight distance and stopping sight distance
38              values are based on the default assumption of level grades, normally intersecting
39              roadways, and with passenger cars as the design vehicle. When deviating from the
40              default assumptions, the engineer shall take the roadway grades, intersection skew,
41              and design vehicle classification into consideration when calculating the required
42              intersection sight distance and/or stopping sight distance.
43              Public roads shall have minimum stopping sight distance, as measured from a
44              height of three and one-half (3½) feet to a target on the roadway nominally two (2)
45              feet in height, in accordance with Table 40.350.030-7. The effect of grades on
46              stopping sight distance shall be calculated using the most current version of the



                                                                                                         213
 1        Washington State Department of Transportation’s Design Manual.
 2        For unposted roadways, the legal maximum speed limit shall be fifty (50) miles per
 3        hour per the “basic rule” under RCW 46.61.400.
 4
                            Table 40.350.030-7. Stopping Sight Distance
                         Speed (mph)                   Minimum Stopping Distance (feet)
                              25                                       155
                              30                                       200
                              35                                       250
                              40                                       305
                              45                                       360
                              50                                       425

 5   b.   Controlled Intersection and Driveway Sight Distance Triangle. Traffic entering an
 6        uncontrolled public road from stop-controlled public roads, or from private roads or private
 7        driveways, shall have minimum intersection sight distances, as shown in Table
 8        40.350.030-8. Sight distance shall be measured from an eye height of three and one-half
 9        (3½) feet above the controlled road pavement surface and fifteen (15) feet from the edge of
10        the vehicle traveled way of the uncontrolled public road. The object height on the
11        uncontrolled public road shall be three and one-half (3½) feet above the pavement surface
12        located four (4) feet to the right of the striped or assumed centerline of the roadway. For
13        multilane highways, the object on the uncontrolled roadway shall be located on the
14        approach lane closest to the controlled side street. Sight distance triangles shall be clear of
15        all obstructions, including, but not limited to, landscaping, fences, structures and earth
16        berms between the heights of three (3) and eight and one-half (8½) feet, as measured from
17        the pavement surface.
